respectively, such that
T_{N}\circ u=u\circ T_{M}
. Assume that
M
and
N
have
h
-slope decompositions for
T_{M}
and
T_{N}
. Then
u(M^{\leq h})\subseteq N^{\leq h}
and
u(M^{>h})\subseteq N^{>h}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{ker}(u)
,
\mathrm{Im}(u)
and
\mathrm{coker}(u)
have
h
-slope decompositions.

Proof.

The inclusions
u(M^{\leq h})\subseteq N^{\leq h}
and
u(M^{>h})\subseteq N^{>h}
are evident from the property that
u
is equivariant. It follows that
\mathrm{ker}(u)=\mathrm{ker}(u)\cap M^{\leq h}\oplus\mathrm{ker}(u)\cap M^{>h}
and this is an
h
-slope decomposition. The remaining points are left to the reader.
∎

5.1.3.
Compact operators

Let
F
be a non archimedean field extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
M\in Ob(\mathbf{Ban}(F))
, and let
T
be a compact endomorphism of
M
. Then by
[
Ser62
]
,
M
has a slope decomposition with respect to
T
. We can generalize this slightly.

Proposition 5.1.4
.

Let
M^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{D}(F)
and let
T\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{D}(F)}(M^{\bullet})
be a compact operator (in the sense of definition
2.4.12
). Then for any
h\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have a direct sum decomposition in
\mathcal{D}(F)
,
M^{\bullet}=M^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus M^{\bullet,>h}
characterized by the property that
the cohomology groups
\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet})
have
h
-slope decompositions for
T
, and
\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet})^{\leq h}=\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet,\leq h})
.

Proof.

We first check that if we have such a decomposition, then it is unique. Indeed, let
M^{\bullet}=M^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus M^{\bullet,>h}=N^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus N^{\bullet,>h}
be two decompositions. Then we see that the map
M^{\bullet,\leq h}\rightarrow N^{\bullet,\leq h}
obtained by composing the inclusion into
M^{\bullet}
and the projection orthogonal to

N^{\bullet,>h}
induces a quasi-isomorphism. We deduce similarly that the map
M^{\bullet,>h}\rightarrow N^{\bullet,>h}
is a quasi-isomorphism. On the other hand, the map
M^{\bullet,\leq h}\rightarrow N^{\bullet,>h}
and
M^{\bullet,>h}\rightarrow N^{\bullet,\leq h}
are the zero map on cohomology, and they are therefore the zero map (since
F
is a field).

Now check the existence. First assume that
T
is represented by a compact morphism in
\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))
. Then, by applying the spectral theory to each term of the complex, we get a direct sum decomposition
M^{\bullet}=M^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus M^{\bullet,>h}
, where for each
i
,
M^{i}=M^{i,\leq h}\oplus M^{i,>h}
is the
h
-slope decomposition of
M^{i}
. Moreover, one deduces from lemma
5.1.2
that
\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet})
has an
h
-slope decomposition with
\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet})^{\leq h}=\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet,\leq h})
,
\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet})^{>h}=\mathrm{H}^{i}(M^{\bullet,>h})
.

Now assume that
M^{\bullet}
is represented by
``\lim_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet}\in Ob(\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))))
and
T
by a compact operator of
``\lim_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet}\in Ob(\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))))
. By lemma
2.4.10
,
T
induces canonically a compact endomorphism
T_{i}
of
M^{\bullet}_{i}
for
i
large enough and there are factorization diagrams:

\textstyle{M^{\bullet}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{i+1}}
\textstyle{M_{i+1}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{i}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{i}}
\textstyle{M_{i}^{\bullet}}

For any
h\in\mathbb{Q}
, we deduce that
M_{i+1}^{\bullet,\leq h}\rightarrow M_{i}^{\bullet,\leq h}
is a quasi-isomorphism. We also deduce that
M_{i+1}^{\bullet,\leq h}\rightarrow M_{i}^{\bullet,>h}
and
M_{i+1}^{\bullet,>h}\rightarrow M_{i}^{\bullet,\leq h}
are the zero map on cohomology. Using that we are over a field, we deduce that these are the zero map. We therefore deduce that
``\lim_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet}=``\lim_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus``\lim_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet,>h}
.
By taking the limit, we deduce that there is a decomposition
M^{\bullet}=M^{\bullet,\leq h}\oplus M^{\bullet,>h}
. We now immediately check that this gives an
h
-slope decomposition on cohomology.
The case where
M^{\bullet}
is represented by
``\mathrm{colim}_{i}"M_{i}^{\bullet}\in Ob(\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))))
is similar and left to the reader.

∎

We have natural projections
M^{\bullet,\leq h^{\prime}}\rightarrow M^{\bullet,\leq h}
for any
h^{\prime}>h\in\mathbb{Q}
. We let
M^{\bullet,fs}=\mathrm{lim}_{h\geq 0}M^{\bullet,\leq h}
be the finite slope part of
M^{\bullet}
. There is a map
M^{\bullet}\rightarrow M^{\bullet,fs}
.

5.1.5.
Action of an algebra

Let
T^{+}
be a commutative monoid (with neutral element) and
T^{++}\subseteq T^{+}
be a sub-semigroup (possibly without neutral element), such that
T^{+}.T^{++}\subseteq T^{++}
. We also assume that for any
t,t^{\prime}\in T^{++}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t^{\prime\prime}\in T^{+}
such that
t^{n}=t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
M\in\mathbf{Ban}(F)
. We assume that we have an algebra action
\mathbb{Z}[T^{+}]\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{F}(M)
, such that the ideal
\mathbb{Z}[T^{++}]
acts by potent compact operators.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in T^{++}
acting compactly on
M
, and any
h\in\mathbb{Q}
, there exists
h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
M^{\leq_{t}h}\subseteq M^{\leq_{t^{\prime}}h^{\prime}}
, where
M^{\leq_{t}h}
and
M^{\leq_{t^{\prime}}h^{\prime}}
are the slope
\leq h
part for
t
and
\leq h^{\prime}
part for
t^{\prime}
respectively.

Proof.

We see that
M^{\leq_{t}h}
is stable under
T^{+}
. In particular it is stable under the action of
t^{\prime}
. Moreover,
t^{\prime}
is invertible since there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t^{\prime\prime}\in T^{+}
such that
t^{n}=t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}
. It follows that
M^{\leq_{t}h}\subseteq M^{\leq_{t^{\prime}}h^{\prime}}
where
h^{\prime}
is the maximum of the valuation of the eigenvalues of
t^{\prime}
on
M^{\leq_{t}h}
.
∎

Let
M^{\bullet}\in Ob(\mathcal{D}(F))
. We assume that we have an algebra action
\mathbb{Z}[T^{+}]\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{D}(F)}(M^{\bullet})
, such that the ideal
\mathbb{Z}[T^{++}]
acts by potent compact operators.

Lemma 5.1.7
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in T^{++}
acting compactly on
M^{\bullet}
, the corresponding finite slope parts
M^{\bullet,t-fs}
and
M^{\bullet,t^{\prime}-fs}
are quasi-isomorphic.

Proof.

We deduce from lemma
5.1.6
, that for any
h\in\mathbb{Q}
, the projection
M^{\bullet}\rightarrow M^{\bullet,\leq_{t}h}
factors through
M^{\bullet}\rightarrow M^{\bullet,\leq_{t^{\prime}}h^{\prime}}
for some
h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Passing to the limit over
h
and
h^{\prime}
we deduce that we have a map
M^{\bullet,t-fs}\rightarrow M^{\bullet,t^{\prime}-fs}
. This map is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

In view of this lemma, we use the notation
M^{\bullet,fs}
to mean
M^{\bullet,t-fs}
for any compact operator
t\in T^{++}
.

5.2.
Correspondences and cohomology with support

We now discuss the action of a cohomological correspondence on the cohomology with support of a sheaf.
Let
(F,\mathcal{O}_{F})
be a non-archimedean local field and let
\mathcal{X}
be an adic space of finite type over
\mathrm{Spa}(F,\mathcal{O}_{F})
. Let

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

be a correspondence, where
\mathcal{C}
is of finite type and
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are morphisms of adic spaces.

5.2.1.
Action of the correspondences on subsets of
\mathcal{X}

Let
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
be the set of subsets of
\mathcal{X}
. Let us denote by
T:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
the map which takes
\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
to
p_{2}(p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}))
and
T^{t}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
the map which takes
\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
to
p_{1}(p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}))
.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

If
p_{1}
is proper (see
[
Hub96
]
, section 1.3), the map
T^{t}
preserves closed subsets and closed subsets with quasi-compact complements. If
p_{1}
is flat, the map
T^{t}
preserves open subsets and quasi-compact open subsets.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a closed subset of
\mathcal{X}
. Then
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{C}
, and
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})
is closed by properness. Moreover if
\mathcal{Z}
has quasi-compact complement, then
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})
has quasi-compact complement. We can find a formal model for the projection
p_{1}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{1}:\mathfrak{C}\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}
, which is proper and with the property that the complement of
p_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{Z}
is the generic fiber of an open subset of
\mathfrak{C}
(
[
L9̈0
]
, thm. 1.6, thm. 3.1). Therefore
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})=\mathrm{sp}^{-1}(Z_{k})
for some closed subset
Z_{k}
of the special fiber of
\mathfrak{C}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is proper,
\mathfrak{p}_{1}(Z_{k})
is closed, and
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})=\mathrm{sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{1}(Z_{k}))
has quasi-compact complement.
Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be an open subset. Then
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
is open in
\mathcal{C}
and
T^{t}(\mathcal{U})
is open since flat morphisms are open (
[
Hub96
]
, lemma 1.7.9). If we assume that
\mathcal{U}
is quasi-compact, then
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
and hence
T^{t}(\mathcal{U})
are as well.
∎

5.2.3.
Action of the correspondence on cohomology

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a sheaf of
\mathscr{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules. Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be an open subset of
\mathcal{X}
and let
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
be a closed subset. We assume that
T(\mathcal{U})
is open and that
p_{1}
is finite flat. We also assume that we have a map
T:p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
. We can define a map

T:\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})

as the following composite:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U}))}(p_{2}^{-1}(T(\mathcal{U})),p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})}(p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}),p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})}(p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}),p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F}),

where:

•

a
is a pull back map along
p_{2}^{-1}(T(\mathcal{U}))\rightarrow T(\mathcal{U})
,

•

b
is a pull back map along
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\rightarrow p_{2}^{-1}(T(\mathcal{U}))
. Notice that
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U}))\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})=p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
,

•

c
is given by the map
T:p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
,

•

d
is given by the trace map of lemma
2.1.2
. Notice that
p_{1}(p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}))\subset T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}
.

Remark 5.2.4
.

We observe that in the definition of the correspondence, the map
p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
is only used in a neighborhood of

p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}).

Indeed, let
\mathcal{V}
be a neighborhood of
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
in
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
, then we have

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})}(p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}),p_{i}^{\star}\mathscr{F})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})}(\mathcal{V},p_{i}^{\star}\mathscr{F})

for
i=1,2
.

5.2.5.
Representing the correspondence by a map of complexes

In this section, we prove under some mild assumptions that the map
T
of the last section can be represented by a morphism of complexes of Banach spaces. We assume that
\mathscr{F}
is a locally projective Banach sheaf.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
and
T(\mathcal{U})
are quasi-compact open subsets, and that
\mathcal{Z}
and
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})
have quasi-compact complements. Then the map
T:\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
is represented by a morphism in
\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))
.

Proof.

We consider finite affinoid
\mathscr{F}
-acyclic coverings
T(\mathcal{U})=\cup_{i\in I}U_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}=\cup_{i\in I^{\prime}}U^{\prime}_{i}
. We also let
Z=\cap_{j\in J}Z_{j}
,
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})=\cap_{j\in J^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}_{j}
where
Z_{j}^{c}
and
(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c}
are affinoid and
\mathscr{F}
-acyclic and
J
,
J^{\prime}
are finite.
We see that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})
is represented by a cone of a map of Čech complexes

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},\mathscr{F})\big)[-1],

and similarly
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
is represented by

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I^{\prime}},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U^{\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},\mathscr{F})\big)[-1].

We now proceed to represent the map
T
.
We first have a map (pullback under
p_{2}
):

A:\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]\rightarrow

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i})\}_{i\in I},p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]

We then have a restriction map, composed with the map
p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
:

B:\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i})\}_{i\in I},p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]\rightarrow

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\}_{i\in I},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}\cap T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1].

We now observe that
\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\}_{i\in I}
is a covering of
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
since
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq p_{2}^{-1}(T(\mathcal{U}))
. We also observe that
\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}\cap T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
is a covering of
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}\cap T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})^{c})
since
p_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq p_{1}^{-1}(T^{t}(\mathcal{Z}))
.

We now pick an
\mathscr{F}
-acyclic finite affinoid covering
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})=\cup_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}}U^{\prime\prime}_{i}
which refines both
\{p_{2}^{-1}U_{i}\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\}_{i\in I}
and
\{p_{1}^{-1}U_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I^{\prime}}
. We also let
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})=\cap_{j\in J^{\prime\prime}}Z^{\prime\prime}_{j}
where
(Z^{\prime\prime}_{j})^{c}
is affinoid and
\mathscr{F}
-acyclic,
J^{\prime\prime}
is finite, and the covering
\cup_{j\in J^{\prime\prime}}(Z^{\prime\prime}_{j})^{c}
of
p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})^{c}
refines both
\{p_{1}^{-1}(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c}\}_{j\in J^{\prime}}
and
\{p_{2}^{-1}(Z_{j})^{c}\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})^{c}\}_{j\in J}
.
We deduce that there are refinement maps:

C:\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\}_{i\in I},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{2}^{-1}(U_{i}\cap Z_{j}^{c})\cap p_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{U}\cap T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I\times J},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]\rightarrow

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime\prime}_{j})^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime\prime}\times J^{\prime\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]

and

D:\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{1}^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{i})\}_{i\in I^{\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{1}^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]\rightarrow

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime\prime}_{j})^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime\prime}\times J^{\prime\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1].

But since
p_{1}
is finite,
p_{1}^{-1}
sends
\mathscr{F}
-acyclic affinoids to
p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
-acyclic affinoids. Hence
D
is actually a quasi-isomorphism, and we can pick an inverse
D^{-1}
for
D
up to homotopy by lemma
2.4.6
.

We finally have a trace map:

E:\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{p_{1}^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{i})\}_{i\in I^{\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{p_{1}^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c})\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]\rightarrow

\mathrm{Cone}\big(\check{C}(\{U^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I^{\prime}},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U^{\prime}_{i}\cap(Z^{\prime}_{j})^{c}\}_{(i,j)\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},\mathscr{F})\big)[-1]

Then
E\circ D^{-1}\circ C\circ B\circ A
represents
T
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.7
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}=\cup_{n}\mathcal{U}_{n}
is a countable union of quasi-compact open subsets. Let
\mathcal{Z}=\cap_{n}\mathcal{Z}_{n}
be a countable intersection of closed subsets with quasi-compact complement. Assume that
T(\mathcal{U}_{n})
is quasi-compact and
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{n})
is the complement of a quasi-compact space. Assume also that
\mathcal{U}
,
T(\mathcal{U})
are a finite union of quasi-Stein spaces and that the complements of
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{T}^{t}(\mathcal{Z})
are finite union of quasi-Stein spaces. Then the map
T:\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
is represented in
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
.

Proof.

We can assume that
\mathcal{U}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{n-1}
.
By lemma
5.2.6
, the maps:
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap T(\mathcal{U}_{n})}(T(\mathcal{U}_{n}),\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{n})\cap\mathcal{U}_{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{n},\mathscr{F})
are representable by maps in
\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))
.
We then pass to the limit over
n
. See also lemma
2.5.21
.
∎

Remark 5.2.8
.

We note that the assumptions of lemma
5.2.6
and corollary
5.2.7
are satisfied if
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are finite flat by lemma
5.2.2
.
In our applications to Shimura varieties, the maps of the (toroidal compactifications of the) correspondences are only finite flat for suitable choices of cone decompositions. However, we will only consider cohomology with support of certain subsets which are “well positioned” with respect to the boundary which will have the consequence that the cohomologies we consider can be defined for any choice of cone decomposition and are further independent of this choice.

5.3.
A formal analytic continuation result

In this section we prove a result which will identify the finite slope part of different cohomologies. This can be seen as an (abstract) generalization of
[
Buz03
]
, thm. 5.2. Let
\mathcal{X}
be an adic space of finite type over
\mathrm{Spa}(F,\mathcal{O}_{F})
, and let
p_{1},p_{2}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a correspondence. We assume that
p_{1}
is finite flat. We also let
\mathscr{F}
be a locally projective Banach sheaf (see definition
2.5.2
), and we assume that the map
T:p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
is compact (see definition
2.5.3
). We recall that when
\mathscr{F}
is a locally free coherent sheaf, any map
p_{2}^{\star}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{1}^{\star}\mathscr{F}
is compact.

5.3.1.
The diamond of a correspondence

We now make the further assumption that
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and
T(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. We can build the following diamond shaped diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}

where the composition of the top and lower triangle
res\circ cores
and
cores\circ res
are equal. We can define an endomorphism of each of the four cohomologies occurring in this diagram by suitably composing
T
with restriction and corestriction maps. By abuse of notation we also call these endomorphisms
T
. One checks immediately that all the maps in the diamond are equivariant for these endomorphisms.

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Assume that all the complexes appearing are objects of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and that the endomorphisms
T
are potent compact. After taking finite slope parts, all the maps in the resulting diamond

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})^{fs}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})^{fs}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})^{fs}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})^{fs}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}

are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

For each map
f
in the diagram, there is another map
g
(the composition of two other arrows in the diagram) so that
gf
and
fg
are the endomorphism
T
on the source and target of
f
. As
T
becomes invertible on the finite slope part, so does
f
.
∎

Remark 5.3.3
.

Under the assumptions of corollary
5.2.7
, the operator
T
is potent compact if the map “restriction-corestriction” obtained by composing the top or bottom arrows of the diamond
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})
is compact (see lemma
2.5.25
for a criterium).

As a corollary, we note the following fact:

Corollary 5.3.4
.

Under the hypothesis of proposition
5.3.2
, let
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
be open and closed subsets, such that
T(\mathcal{U})\cap\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}\cap T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. Assume also that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
is an object of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
. Then the operator
T
is well defined and potent compact on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
and the finite slope part of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
is canonically quasi-isomorphic to the finite slope part of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

Under our assumptions, we have restriction-corestriction maps:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap T(\mathcal{U}))\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U})\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})

and

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F}).

We can build the following diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and
T
is therefore well defined and potent compact on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
. We deduce that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})^{fs}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap T(\mathcal{U})}(T(\mathcal{U}),\mathscr{F})^{fs}
which is in turn quasi-isomorphic to
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})^{fs}
.
∎

5.3.5.
The infinite diamond

It is interesting to iterate the operator
T
. We now work under the stronger assumption that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are finite flat. These assumptions imply that for any
n\geq 0
, the
n
-th iterate
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
of the correspondence
\mathcal{C}
comes with two finite flat projections
p_{1,n}
and
p_{2,n}
.

Remark 5.3.6
.

Later we will apply this material to toroidal compactifications of Shimura varieties. We therefore have to work under slightly more general assumptions. Namely, it is not possible in general to find cone decompositions such that all the maps between compactifications of Hecke correspondences are finite flat. Nevertheless, by allowing suitable changes of the cone decompositions, we can always assume that a given map is finite flat. Moreover, the composition of compactified Hecke correspondences and their action on the cohomology has been explained in detail in section
4.2.2
. Therefore, for the clarity of the exposition, we will keep the assumption that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are finite flat here.

We let
\mathcal{U}_{m}=T^{m}(\mathcal{U})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{n}=(T^{t})^{n}(\mathcal{Z})
. We assume that
T(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and
T^{t}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. The sequences
\{\mathcal{U}_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
and
\{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
are therefore decreasing.

We can then construct diamonds as above for all
n,m\geq 0
:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m+1}}(\mathcal{U}_{m+1},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n+1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n+1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m+1}}(\mathcal{U}_{m+1},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}

and we can add them to get an infinite diamond diagram looking like:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}}(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}}(\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}}(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{cores}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

We assume that all the objects of the above diagram belong to
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
. We now make the further assumption that there exists
(m_{0},n_{0})
such that one morphism “restriction-corestriction” obtained by composing the top or low arrows of a diamond
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n_{0}+1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m_{0}}}(\mathcal{U}_{m_{0}},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n_{0}}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m_{0}+1}}(\mathcal{U}_{m_{0}+1},\mathscr{F})
is compact.

For any
m,n
with
m,n\geq 0
and
(m,n)\neq(0,0)
, we can define an endomorphism
T_{m,n}:\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathscr{F})
by composing
T
,
res
and
cores
in a suitable order. We abuse notation and denote this operator by
T
. The operator
T
is potent compact because some power of it will factor over the compact “restriction-corestriction” map above. In any case, we can speak of the finite slope direct factor of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{U}_{m}}(\mathcal{U}_{m},\mathscr{F})
for
T
.

Theorem 5.3.7
.

On the finite slope part, all the morphisms of the infinite diamond are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

This follows from proposition
5.3.2
.
∎

Corollary 5.3.8
.

Under the hypothesis of theorem
5.3.7
, assume that there is
m,n,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
(T^{t})^{n+s}(\mathcal{Z})\cap T^{m}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq(T^{t})^{n}(\mathcal{Z})
is a closed subset and
T^{m+s}(\mathcal{U})\cap(T^{t})^{n}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq T^{m}(\mathcal{U})
. Assume moreover that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
is an object of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
. Then the operator
T^{s}
is well defined and potent compact on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
and the finite slope part of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathscr{F})
is canonically quasi-isomorphic to the finite slope part of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

This follows from corollary
5.3.4
.
∎

5.4.
Overconvergent cohomologies

Let
(G,X)
be an abelian type Shimura datum such that
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is quasi split.
Let
w\in{}^{M}W
. For a choice of
+
or
-
and a weight
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
we want to define finite slope overconvergent cohomologies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa)^{\pm,fs}
and their cuspidal counterparts
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
by taking cohomologies with suitable support conditions of neighborhoods of the inverse image under the Hodge-Tate period map of
\mathcal{P_{\mu}}\backslash\mathcal{P_{\mu}}wK_{p}
, and by taking finite slope parts for a suitable Hecke operator. We will also define variants
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
where
\chi:T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to{\overline{F}}^{\times}
is a finite order character.

5.4.1.
First definition

For a level
K_{p}=K_{p,m^{\prime},b}
with
m^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
and
m^{\prime}>0
and a weight
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
, we define:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{+,fs}:=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{+,fs}.

Implicit in this definition is that this cohomology is an object of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{+}
acts on it in a way that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{++}
acts by potent compact operators (this will be proved below in Theorem
5.4.3
).

Similarly for a weight
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
, we define:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{-,fs}:=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}.

Again implicit in this definition is that this cohomology is an object of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{-}
acts on it in a way that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{--}
acts by potent compact operators (this will also be proved below in Theorem
5.4.3
).

We have similar definitions for cuspidal cohomology.

5.4.2.
Existence of finite slope cohomology

Theorem 5.4.3
.

Let
K_{p}=K_{p,m,b}
for some
m\geq b\geq 0
with
m>0
, and fix
w\in{}^{M}W
and
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
.

(1)

There is an action of
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{+}
on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
for which
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{++}
acts via potent compact operators. The same statement holds for
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))
.

(2)

There is an action of
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{-}
on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
for which
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{--}
acts via potent compact operators. The same statement holds for
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))
.

Proof.

We only prove point
1
for non cuspidal cohomology. The rest of the argument is very similar and left to the reader.

Let
U=(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[)
and
Z=(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})
. By lemma
3.3.21
we have

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{U\cap Z}(U,\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}).

Now if
T
is the Hecke operator
[K_{p}tK_{p}]
for any
t\in T^{+}
, we have
T(U)\subseteq U
and
T^{t}(Z)\subseteq Z
by lemma
3.5.10
and hence there is an action of
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{+}
on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{U\cap Z}(U,\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
via the construction explained at the beginning of Section
5.3
. That this action defines an action of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{+}
follows from the discussion of section
4.2.2
.

Now suppose that
T
is associated to
t\in T^{++}
. In order to simplify notations, we choose
t
such that
\min(t)=\inf_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}v(\alpha(t))\geq 1
. In order to show the action of
T
is potent compact, it suffices to show that the “restriction-corestriction” map

\mathrm{R\Gamma}_{U\cap T^{t}(Z)}(U,\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathrm{R\Gamma}_{T(U)\cap Z}(T(U),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

is compact. We need to check the assumptions of lemma
2.5.25
.

We will be done if we can find a quasi-compact open subset
U^{\prime}
such that
T(U)\cap Z\subseteq U^{\prime}
and
\overline{U^{\prime}}\subseteq U
, and closed subset
Z_{1},Z_{2}
, with quasi-compact complement, such that
U\cap T^{t}(Z)\subseteq Z_{1}
,
Z_{1}\subseteq\overset{\circ}{Z}_{2}
and
Z_{2}\subseteq Z
.
By lemma
3.5.10
, we have:

U\cap T^{t}(Z)\subseteq(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}\big(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{1}}K_{p}\big)

and

T(U)\cap Z\subseteq(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}\big(]C_{w,k}[_{1,\overline{0}}K_{p}\big)

We first find
U^{\prime}
.
By lemma
3.3.21
,

(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}\big(]C_{w,k}[_{0,-\infty}K_{p}\big)\subseteq U.

We may now take
U_{1}
a quasi-compact open of
\mathcal{FL}
with the property that
]C_{w,k}[_{1,\overline{0}}\subseteq U_{1}\subseteq]C_{w,k}[_{\frac{1}{2},-1}
and let
U^{\prime}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(U_{1}K_{p})
. Observe that
U_{1}K_{p}
is a finite union of translates of
U_{1}
. It follows easily from lemma
3.3.21
, (1) that
U^{\prime}
has the required property.

We now proceed to find
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
. By lemma
3.3.21
,

(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,b}}^{tor})^{-1}\big(]C_{w,k}[_{-\infty,{0}}\big)\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[).

We can find
]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{1}}\subseteq W_{1}\subseteq W_{2}\subseteq]C_{w,k}[_{-\infty,{0}}
with
W_{1}
,
W_{2}
closed with quasi-compact complements and
W_{1}\subseteq\overset{\circ}{W}_{2}
. We deduce that
]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{1}}K_{p}\subseteq W_{1}K_{p}\subseteq W_{2}K_{p}\subseteq]Y_{w,k}[
and that
W_{1}K_{p}\subseteq\overset{\circ}{W_{2}K_{p}}=\overset{\circ}{W_{2}}K_{p}
since
W_{1}K_{p}
and
W_{2}K_{p}
are a finite union of translates of
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. We let
Z_{1}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(W_{1}K_{p})
and
Z_{2}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(W_{2}K_{p})
.
We let
Z_{1}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,b}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{\cup_{s<\frac{1}{2}}]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},s}}K_{p,m,b})
. We now find
Z_{2}
. We first observe that
\overline{\cup_{s<\frac{1}{2}}]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},s}}\subseteq]C^{w}_{k}[\subseteq]X^{w}_{k}[
, and that
\overline{]X^{w}_{k}[}=\mathrm{sp}^{-1}(X^{w}_{k})\subseteq]Y_{w,k}[
. Since
]X^{w}_{k}[K_{p,m,b}
is a finite union of translates of
]X^{w}_{k}[
, we can take

Z_{2}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,b}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]X^{w}_{k}[}K_{p,m,b}).

We observe that
Z_{1}^{c}\cap(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,0}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[)
is a non-empty quasi-compact open, call it
W
. We can find a non-empty quasi-compact open
W^{\prime}
with the property that
\overline{W}\subset W
.
We let

Z^{\prime}=]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},\overline{0}}

and
Z=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m,0}}^{tor})^{-1}(Z^{\prime})
.

∎

5.4.4.
The finite slope cohomology and the cohomology of dagger spaces

We construct two exact triangles which give an interpretation of finite slope cohomology for a given
w\in{}^{M}W
as being quasi-isomorphic to the cone of a map between finite slope cohomology of certain dagger spaces, or the cone of a map between cohomology with compact support of dagger spaces. This will be useful for duality and vanishing.

Proposition 5.4.5
.

Let
K_{p}=K_{p,m,b}
for some
m\geq b\geq 0
with
m>0
, and fix
w\in{}^{M}W
and
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
.

(1)

The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{-}
acts on

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and the ideal
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{--}
acts by potent compact operators. We have an exact triangle:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{-,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

(2)

The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{+}
acts on

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and the ideal
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{++}
acts by potent compact operators. We have an exact triangle:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{+,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{+,fs}

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{+,fs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

Proof.

We have an exact triangle:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

since
]Y_{w,k}[=]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[\coprod]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},{0}}
by lemma
3.3.22
.
We also have an exact triangle

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{{0},\overline{0}})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

since
\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}=\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[}\coprod]C_{w,k}[_{{0},\overline{0}}
by lemma
3.3.22
, and
]X_{w,k}[
is an open neighborhood of
]C_{w,k}[_{{0},\overline{0}}
in
\mathcal{FL}\setminus\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[}
.
It therefore remains to prove that the relevant Hecke algebras act in an equivariant way on the triangle and to take the finite slope part.
We observe that the cohomologies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
are represented by complexes in
\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F))
. It follows from lemma
3.5.10
, (2) and (7) that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{-}
is acting and that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{--}
is acting via potent compact operators.
We observe that the cohomologies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
are represented by complexes in
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and it follows form lemma
3.5.10
(2) and (7) that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{+}
is acting and that
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{++}
is acting via potent compact operators.
∎

Remark 5.4.6
.

One could also consider two other triangles: one for the
-
theory, using cohomology with support in the closed subsets
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}<w}X_{w^{\prime},k}[})
and
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]X_{w,k}[})
, and one for the
+
theory using cohomology of the open subsets
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{w^{\prime}<w}X_{w^{\prime},k}[})
and
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]X_{w,k}[})
.

By remark
2.7.2
, the cohomologies

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}),~\textrm{and}~\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

are the cohomologies with compact support of the dagger spaces

(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})^{\dagger}~\textrm{and}~(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]Y_{w,k}[})^{\dagger}.

Similarly,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}),~\textrm{and}~\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

are the cohomologies of the dagger spaces

(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})^{\dagger}~\textrm{and}~(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]Y_{w,k}[})^{\dagger}.

Proposition 5.4.7
.

The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{-}
acts on

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and the ideal
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{--}
acts by potent compact operators.
Moreover, the morphisms:

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

induce isomorphisms on the finite slope part:

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

Proof.

The cohomologies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
are represented by complexes in
\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
. The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{-}
acts and the operators are potent compact as a consequence of lemma
3.5.10
(2) and (7). The same lemma shows the quasi-isomorphism on the finite slope part.
∎

Corollary 5.4.8
.

We have an exact triangle:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{-,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{-,fs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

Proof.

This is a consequence of propositions
5.4.5
and
5.4.7
.
∎

5.4.9.
Change of support condition

It is important to us that the cohomology
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{\pm,fs}
can actually be realized as the finite slope part of cohomology groups with different support conditions. The following definition is motivated by Lemma
2.5.21
and the discussion of Section
5.3
, especially Corollary
5.3.8
. We start by fixing an element
t\in T^{++}
such that
\min(t)\geq 1
. We let
C=\mathrm{max}(t)
.

Definition 5.4.10
.

Let
m^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
with
m^{\prime}>0
. A
(+,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support is a pair
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{Z})
where:

(1)

\mathcal{U}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\Sigma}^{tor}
which is a finite union of quasi-Stein open subsets.

(2)

\mathcal{Z}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\Sigma}^{tor}
whose complement is a finite union of quasi-Stein open subsets.

(3)

There exists
m,n,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{m,\overline{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{n+s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{{0},C\overline{n}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),

(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{m+s,\overline{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{n}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{Cm,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}).

Let
m^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
with
m^{\prime}>0
. A
(-,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support is a pair
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{Z})
where:

(1)

\mathcal{U}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\Sigma}^{tor}
which is a finite union of quasi-Stein open subsets.

(2)

\mathcal{Z}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\Sigma}^{tor}
whose complement is a finite union of quasi-Stein open subsets.

(3)

There exists
m,n,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{m+s},{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},n}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{C\overline{m},{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),

(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{m},0}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},n+s}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{-1,Cn}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}).

Example 5.4.11
.

For any
m^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
with
m^{\prime}>0
and any
s\geq 0
, the pair

\mathcal{U}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\quad\mathcal{Z}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

is a
(+,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support (since
]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\subseteq]X_{w,k}[\cap\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}=]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{0}}
). Similarly, the pair

\mathcal{U}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{-1,s}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\quad\mathcal{Z}=(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{s},\overline{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

is a
(-,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support.

Theorem 5.4.12
.

Let
m^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
with
m^{\prime}>0
, and fix
w\in{}^{M}W
and
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
.

(1)

Let
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{Z})
be a
(+,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support condition. Then
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))
are objects of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and carry a canonically defined, potent compact action of
T^{s}
where
T=[K_{p,m^{\prime},b}tK_{p,m^{\prime},b}]
be the corresponding Hecke operator. . Moreover there are canonical isomorphisms

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa)^{+,fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{T^{s}-fs}

and

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa,cusp)^{+,fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{T^{s}-fs}.

(2)

Let
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{Z})
be a
(-,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support condition. Then
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))
are objects of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
and carry a canonically defined, potent compact action of
T^{s}
where
T=[K_{p,m^{\prime},b}tK_{p,m^{\prime},b}]
. Moreover there are canonical isomorphisms

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa)^{-,fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{T^{s}-fs}

and

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa,cusp)^{-,fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{T^{s}-fs}.

Proof.

We will only prove the first point for non cuspidal cohomology. It follows from lemma
2.5.21
that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{U}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
is an object of
\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{K}^{proj}(\mathbf{Ban}(F)))
.

For the rest of the statement we will use the infinite diamond construction of section
5.3.5
and corollary
5.3.8
. For
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we let
U_{m}=(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(T^{m}(]X_{w,k}[))
and
Z_{n}=(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}((T^{t})^{n}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}))
. Since we have already checked in the proof of theorem
5.4.3
that the “restriction-corestriction” map

\mathrm{R\Gamma}_{U_{0}\cap Z_{1}}(U_{0},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\rightarrow\mathrm{R\Gamma}_{U_{1}\cap Z_{0}}(U_{1},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

is compact, it follows that the conclusion of corollary
5.3.8
hold.

By lemma
3.5.10
we have:

\displaystyle U_{m}\cap Z_{n+s}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{{m},\overline{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{n+s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

\displaystyle U_{m+s}\cap Z_{n}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{{m+s},\overline{0}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{n}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

\displaystyle(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{{m}C,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle U_{m}

\displaystyle(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{0,\overline{n}C}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle Z_{n}.

It follows from corollary
5.3.8
that if
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{Z})
is a
(+,w,K_{p,m^{\prime},b})
-allowed support condition, then
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
carries a canonical action of
T^{s}
and we have canonical quasi-isomorphisms

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{T^{s}-fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{U\cap Z}(U,\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})^{T-fs}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa)^{+,fs}.

∎

5.4.13.
Change of level

Now we investigate how the finite slope cohomologies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa)^{\pm,fs}
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
vary with the level
K_{p}
.

Theorem 5.4.14
.

(1)

For all
w\in{}^{M}W
and all
m^{\prime}\geq m\geq b\geq 0
with
m>0
, the pullback map

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa)^{+,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa)^{+,fs}

and the trace map

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\kappa)^{-,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa)^{-,fs}

are quasi-isomorphisms, compatible with the action of
\mathbb{Q}[T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/T_{b}]
, and the same statements are true for cuspidal cohomology.

(2)

For all
w\in{}^{M}W
and all
m\geq b^{\prime}\geq b\geq 0
with
m>0
, the pullback map

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa)^{+,fs}\rightarrow(\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b^{\prime}},\kappa)^{+,fs})^{T_{b}/T_{b^{\prime}}}

and the trace map

(\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b^{\prime}},\kappa)^{-,fs})^{T_{b}/T_{b^{\prime}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa)^{-,fs}

are quasi-isomorphisms, compatible with the action of
\mathbb{Q}[T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/T_{b}]
, and the same statements are true for cuspidal cohomology.

Proof.

This follows from lemma
4.2.14
.
∎

As a result of the theorem, we can let
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
denote
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa)^{\pm,fs}[\chi]
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p,m,b},\kappa,cusp)^{\pm,fs}[\chi]
for any
m\geq b\geq\mathrm{cond}(\chi)
with
m>0
, as these spaces have been canonically identified.

5.5.
The spectral sequence associated with the Bruhat stratification

Recall that there is a length function
\ell:{}^{M}W\rightarrow[0,d]
where
d=\dim\mathcal{FL}=\dim S_{K}
with the property that
\ell(w)=\dim C_{w}
. We let
\ell_{+}(w)=\ell(w)
and
\ell_{-}(w)=d-\ell(w)
.

The main result of this section is the following theorem:

Theorem 5.5.1
.

Let
\kappa\in X^{\star}(T^{c})^{M_{\mu},+}
be a weight and let
\chi:T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to{\overline{F}}^{\times}
be a finite order character. For a choice of
+
or
-
, there is a
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{\pm}
-equivariant spectral sequence
\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm}
converging to classical finite slope cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}
,
such that

\mathbf{E}_{1}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm}=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=p}\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}.

There are also spectral sequences
\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}
converging to
\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
such that

\mathbf{E}_{1}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=p}\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}.

5.5.2.
Construction of a filtration

We consider the following two stratifications of the special fiber of the flag variety
FL_{k}
, the first one is by open subsets:

\{FL_{k}^{\geq r}=\cup_{\ell(w)\geq r}C_{w,k}\}_{0\leq r\leq d}

and the second one is by closed subsets:

\{FL_{k}^{\leq_{d-r}}=\cup_{\ell(w)\leq d-r}C_{w,k}\}_{0\leq r\leq d}.

We then define
Z^{+}_{r}=\overline{]FL_{k}^{\geq r}[}
and
Z^{-}_{r}=\overline{]FL_{k}^{\leq d-r}[}
. This gives two filtrations

\mathcal{FL}=Z^{\pm}_{0}\supset Z^{\pm}_{1}\supset\cdots\supset Z^{\pm}_{d}\supset Z^{\pm}_{d+1}=\emptyset

by closed subspaces invariant under the Iwahori
\mathrm{Iw}
.

Now let
K_{p}=K_{p,m,b}
for some
m\geq b\geq 0
with
m>0
. We can consider the pullback of these filtrations by
\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}}
to get two filtrations
\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma}=\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{0}\supset\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{1}\supset\cdots\supset\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{d}\supset\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{d+1}=\emptyset
by closed subspaces.

For any weight
\kappa
, we can consider the associated spectral sequence (see section
2.3
):

\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\Rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}).

By definition and lemma
3.3.19
,

\mathrm{H}^{\star}_{\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})=

\mathrm{H}^{\star}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{\ell(w)\geq p}C_{w}[}\cap{]\cup_{\ell(w)\leq p}C_{w}[})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{\ell(w)\leq p}C_{w}[)},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}).

and

\mathrm{H}^{\star}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p}},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})=

\mathrm{H}^{\star}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{\ell(w)\leq d-p}C_{w}[}\cap{]\cup_{\ell(w)\geq d-p}C_{w}[})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}({]\cup_{\ell(w)\geq d-p}C_{w}[)},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}).

We also have a cuspidal version

\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\Rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D)).

We relate the
\mathbf{E}_{1}
pages of these spectral sequences to the overconvergent cohomologies considered in the previous section.

Lemma 5.5.3
.

For all
p
,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})=

\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,~\ell(w)=p}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[\cap\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]X_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})=

\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,~\ell(w)=d-p}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[\cap\overline{]X_{w,k}[})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

We have similar results for cuspidal cohomology.

Proof.

We have

\overline{]\cup_{\ell(w)\geq p}C_{w}[}\cap{]\cup_{\ell(w)\leq p}C_{w}[}=\cup_{w,\ell(w)=p}\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}\cap]X_{w,k}[

and

\overline{]\cup_{\ell(w)\leq d-p}C_{w}[}\cap{]\cup_{\ell(w)\geq d-p}C_{w}[}=\cup_{w,\ell(w)=d-p}{]Y_{w,k}[}\cap\overline{]X_{w,k}[}

by lemma
3.3.20
. The conclusion follows from lemma
2.1.1
.
∎

Lemma 5.5.4
.

For a choice of
+
or
-
,
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{\pm}
acts on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
, and the spectral sequence

\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\Rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})

is
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{\pm}
-equivariant. The same result holds for cuspidal cohomology.

Proof.

Easy and left to the reader.
∎

5.5.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.5.1

For a choice of
\pm
, we have constructed a spectral sequence

\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p}/\mathcal{Z}^{\pm}_{p+1}}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})\Rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K,\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}).

The Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{p,m,b}^{\pm}
acts on this spectral sequence and it makes sense to take the finite slope part by lemma
5.5.4
. Applying the finite slope projector, we obtain the spectral sequence of the theorem.

5.5.6.
Cousin complexes

We now extract from the spectral sequence certain complexes that play a prominent role, in particular in view of corollaries
5.7.7
and
5.8.1
.
We let
\mathcal{C}ous(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm}
be the complex
\mathbf{E}_{1}^{\bullet,0}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm}
(
w_{0}^{M}
is the longest element of
{}^{M}W
):

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{Id/w_{0}^{M}}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=1}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow

\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=2}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{d}_{w_{0}^{M}/Id}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{\pm,fs}

and we let
\mathcal{C}ous(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}
be the complex
\mathbf{E}_{1}^{\bullet,0}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}
:

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{Id/w_{0}^{M}}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=1}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow

\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=2}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{d}_{w_{0}^{M}/Id}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}

5.6.
Cohomological vanishing

The following vanishing theorem is crucial in order to study the spectral sequence.

Theorem 5.6.1
.

The cohomology complex
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
has amplitude
[0,\ell_{\pm}(w)]
.

Proof.

We only give the argument for the
+
case, as the
-
case follows with minor modifications. Let
b
be the conductor of
\chi
. We can realize
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,cusp)^{+,fs}
as the
\chi
-isotypic part of the finite slope part of

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}\cap C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

for any
s\geq 0
and
m^{\prime}\geq b
,
m^{\prime}>0
by example
5.4.11
and theorem
5.4.12
.

We fix
s
large enough so that
\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1})
is quasi-Stein in the minimal compactification. We also fix
m^{\prime}=s
. To simplify notations, we let
K_{p}=K_{p,m^{\prime},b}
.

We observe that under these assumptions,
]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p}\cap]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{0},\overline{s}}K_{p}=]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p}
by lemma
3.3.17
, and therefore the above cohomology writes

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D)).

We shall prove that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

has cohomological amplitude
[0,\ell_{+}(w)]
.

Projecting via
\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma}:\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma}^{tor}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{\star}
and using theorem
4.1.7
, this cohomology is quasi-isomorphic to

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

Le
K^{\prime}_{p}
be the principal level
m^{\prime}
congruence subgroup and let
\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}:\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K^{\prime}_{p}}^{\star}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p}}^{\star}
. Since

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))=

\mathrm{R}\Gamma\big(K_{p}/K^{\prime}_{p},\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\big)

It will suffice to prove that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p})}((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

has cohomological amplitude in
[0,\ell_{+}(w)]
.

Since
]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p}
is a finite disjoint union of translates of
\{]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}k_{i}\}
for elements
k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}
by lemma
3.3.16
, we are left to prove that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}k_{i})}((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}k_{i}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

for
1\leq i\leq n
has cohomological amplitude in
[0,\ell_{+}(w)]
. Also, using the action of
K_{p}
it suffices to treat the case
k_{i}=1
.
The cohomology fits in a distinguished triangle:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}})}((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\rightarrow

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\rightarrow

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}\setminus{]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

Since
\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}}^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}})
is quasi-Stein in the minimal compactification
\mathcal{S}^{\star}_{K^{p}K^{\prime}_{p}}
we have that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

is concentrated in degree
0
.

We will now prove that
\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}}^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}\setminus{]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}})
admits a covering by
\ell_{+}(w)
acyclic spaces. This will show that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}})^{-1}({]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}\setminus{]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}}),\pi_{K^{\prime}_{p},K_{p}}^{\star}(\pi_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma})_{\star}\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

has only cohomology in degree
0
to
\ell_{+}(w)-1
and the theorem will follow.

We recall from corollary
3.3.5
the isomorphism:

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{+}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\times\prod_{\alpha\in(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{-}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}^{o}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle]C_{w,k}[_{\mathcal{FL}}

\displaystyle(u_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle w\prod_{\alpha}u_{\alpha}

Let us fix coordinates
1+u_{\alpha}
on each of the one parameter groups. For these coordinates, the equation of
{]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}\setminus]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}
is:

•

\forall\alpha\in(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{+}
,
|u_{\alpha}|\leq|p^{s-1}|
,

•

\forall\alpha\in(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{-}
,
\exists\epsilon>0
,
|u_{\alpha}|\leq|p^{s+\epsilon}|
,

•

\exists\alpha\in(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{+}
,
\exists\nu>0
,
|u_{\alpha}|\geq|p^{s-\nu}|
.

Since
\sharp(w^{-1}\Phi^{-,M})\cap\Phi^{+}=\ell(w)=\ell_{+}(w)
, we deduce that
{]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}}\setminus{]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}}
is indeed covered by
\ell(w)
acyclic spaces and the same holds for its pre-image by
\pi_{HT,K^{\prime}_{p}}
.
∎

Proposition 5.6.2
.

For the spectral sequence
\mathbf{E}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}
converging to
\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}
we have
\mathbf{E}_{1}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}=0
if
q>0
. In particular
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}^{p,q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}=\mathrm{Gr}^{p}(\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs})=0
if
p<p+q
.

Proof.

This follows from theorem
5.6.1
. The picture of the first page of the spectral sequence is as follows (
w_{0}^{M}
is the longest element of
{}^{M}W
):

\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{0}_{Id/w_{0}^{M}}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=1}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=2}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=1}\mathrm{H}^{0}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=2}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell_{\pm}(w)=2}\mathrm{H}^{0}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

∎

5.7.
Duality

In this section we investigate Serre duality on overconvergent cohomologies.

Theorem 5.7.1
.

For all
w\in{}^{M}W
, there is a pairing:

\langle,\rangle:\mathrm{H}^{i}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{\mp,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}

such that for
t\in T^{\pm}
we have
\langle t-,-\rangle=\langle-,t^{-1}-\rangle
. This pairing induces a pairing between the spectral sequences:

\langle,\rangle_{p,q,r}:\mathbf{E}^{p,q}_{r}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm}\times\mathbf{E}^{d-p,-q}_{r}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{\mp}\rightarrow\overline{F}

On the abutment of the spectral sequence the pairing
\langle,\rangle_{p,q,\infty}
is induced by the perfect Serre duality pairing:

\mathrm{H}^{p+q}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-p-q}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{\mp,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}.

Proof.

We construct the pairing

\langle,\rangle:\mathrm{H}^{i}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{+,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{-,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}.

Choose
b
so that
\chi
is trivial on
T_{b}
. We can realize
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{+,fs}
as the
\chi
-isotypic part of the finite slope part of

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

for any
s>0
and
m^{\prime}\geq b
,
m^{\prime}>s
. by example
5.4.11
and theorem
5.4.12
.

We can realize
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{-,fs}
as the
\chi^{-1}
-isotypic part of the finite slope part of

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{s+1},s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{-1,s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa}).

We have a cup-product by proposition
2.2.1
:

\mathrm{H}^{i}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\times

\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{s+1},s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{-1,s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})

\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{d}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{s+1},\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}}(-D))

and there is a trace map (by theorem
2.7.1
):

\mathrm{H}^{d}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}}^{tor})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{\overline{s+1},\overline{s}}K_{p,m^{\prime},b})}((\pi^{tor}_{HT,K_{p,m^{\prime},b}})^{-1}(]C_{w,k}[_{s,s-1}K_{p,m^{\prime},b}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}}(-D))\rightarrow F.

This pairing intertwines the actions of
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}_{p,m^{\prime},b}^{-}
. It is straightforward (but painful) to check that the induced pairing

\langle,\rangle:\mathrm{H}^{i}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{+,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{-,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}

is independent of choices.

The rest of the theorem follows from the functoriality of the trace map.

∎

We now prove the following theorem:

Theorem 5.7.2
.

The pairing

\langle,\rangle:\mathrm{H}^{i}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{\pm,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{\mp,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}

is non degenerate. (Equivalently it induces perfect pairings between the finite dimensional generalized eigenspaces for
T^{\pm}
).

Proof.

We consider the pairing
\langle,\rangle:\mathrm{H}^{i}_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi,cusp)^{+,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{w}(K^{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa,\chi^{-1})^{-,fs}\rightarrow\overline{F}
. The other case is similar.
Recall from propositions
5.4.5
and corollary
5.4.8
, the exact triangles:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa)^{-,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})^{-,fs}

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})^{-,fs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

and

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w^{\prime},k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{+,fs}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{+,fs}

\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p}K_{p},\kappa,cusp)^{+,fs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

Using the naturality of the duality pairing, it is sufficient to show that the pairings:

\mathrm{H}^{i}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})^{-,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{+,fs}\rightarrow F

and

\mathrm{H}^{i}((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})^{-,fs}\times\mathrm{H}^{d-i}_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}^{tor}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))^{+,fs}\rightarrow F

are non degenerate.
We only explain how to show that the first pairing is non-degenerate. The proof for the second pairing is identical. We take a finite affinoid covering
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[)=\cup_{i\in I}U_{i}
.

We deduce that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma((\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[}),\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})
, which is the cohomology of the dagger space
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[)^{\dagger}
, is represented by the Čech complex:

M^{\bullet}:\prod_{i}\mathrm{H}^{0}(U_{i}^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow\prod_{i,j}\mathrm{H}^{0}((U_{i}\cap U_{j})^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow\cdots

We deduce that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]\cup_{w^{\prime}>w}Y_{w,k}[})}(\mathcal{S}_{K^{p}K_{p},\Sigma}^{tor},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa})
(which is the cohomology with compact support of the dagger space
(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(]Y_{w,k}[)^{\dagger}
) is represented by the Čech complex:

N^{\bullet}:\cdots\rightarrow\prod_{i,j}\mathrm{H}^{d}_{c}((U_{i}\cap U_{j})^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\rightarrow\prod_{i}\mathrm{H}^{d}_{c}(U_{i}^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))

Using the duality for affinoid dagger spaces (see theorem
2.7.1
), we see that
M^{\bullet}
is a complex of inductive limit of Banach spaces,
N^{\bullet}
is a complex of projective limit of Banach spaces and these complexes are strict dual of each other. More precisely, the Serre pairing is induced by the perfect duality on each of the modules (which identifies each module as the strong dual of the other):

\mathrm{H}^{d}_{c}((U_{J})^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{\kappa}(-D))\times\mathrm{H}^{0}((U_{J})^{\dagger},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow F,

where
J\subseteq I
and
U_{J}=\cap_{j\in J}U_{j}
.
In general, this does not induce a perfect duality between the cohomology of
M^{\bullet}
and
N^{\bullet}
. However, we will prove that this is the case on the finite slope part. Now, let
t\in T^{--}
be an operator acting compactly. We claim that we can represent it by an endomorphism of
M^{\bullet}
.
Indeed, for all
i
we can find an affinoid
V_{i}
with
\overline{U}_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
(by
[
L9̈0
]
, thm. 5.1). Let
V=\cup V_{i}
. By shrinking the
V_{i}
’s, we may assume by lemma
3.5.10
, 7), that
t(V)\subseteq(\pi_{HT,K_{p}}^{tor})^{-1}(\overline{]Y_{w,k}[})\subseteq V
. Let us finally take an affinoid covering
U^{\prime}_{i}
refining
t(V)\cap U_{i}
.
By lemma
5.2.6
, there is a map

t:\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\prime}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{V_{i}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa}).

There are restriction maps
A:\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\dagger}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\prime}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})
and

B:\check{C}(\{V_{i}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})\rightarrow\check{C}(\{U_{i}^{\dagger}\},\mathcal{V}_{-2\rho_{nc}-w_{0,M}\kappa})
. We deduce that
B\circ t\circ A
represents
t
acting on
M^{\bullet}
(and is compact since
A
and
B
are easily seen to be compact).
We easily deduce that the adjoint of
t
represents
t^{-1}
acting on
N^{\bullet}
.
Passing to the slope
\leq h
part on both
M^{\bullet}
and
N^{\bullet}
, we obtain a perfect pairing between complexes of finite dimensional vector spaces. It induces a perfect pairing on slope
\leq h
cohomology groups.
∎

Using duality, we can prove vanishing theorems for non-cuspidal cohomology:
